, Nothing could be more satisfactory in the

salvation than the result of the mass mecting 
ef the Canton-Hankow Railway Company's 
shareholders, which was. held with the object 
of electing a- president, vice-president and 
committee of management. Amid evidences 
of fiaternal amiability and general good-will 
the ‘much canvassed meeting, which, was 
expected by some to end in dissension and 
uproar, passed off peacefully, regularly and 
as composedly'as the hall-yearly meeting ‘of 
the Hongko*g and Shanghai Banking. Cor- 
poration, Eight hundred shareholders pre- 
sented their tickets of admission, Ijstened to. 
the speeches with decorum, and voted for 
‘the men. they considered best qualified 
to represent the general interests of the in- 
vestora on the board of management, After 
‘all that “has occurred in the past, after 
the riots, tumults, street fracas which have 
invariably followed, the meetings which had’ 
previously been held with a similar object 
in vidw, and the arrest of .prominent share- 
holders who have.been kept in gaol for con- 
siderable periods because of their connection 
with the: moré=vigorous clement,: is |t not 
Feasonable.to wonder. what genius succeeded. 
‘in stilling the storm and, placating the im: 
placable?. Why. should it have taken all 
these weary months to'arrive. at: a decision 
which was reached with practical unanimity 
-the-othor-day?What-bas happened in_| 
meantime to remove the tension, ‘and tone, 
down the spirit of suspicion with which all 
parties, sections and cliques seemed to be 
tom and tormented at.the earlier meetings ? 
If the Canton-Hankow Railway Co. had been 
avwild-cat affalr, floated for the benefit ofafew 
scheming financiers, or at the instance of the

‘returns being: derived from its operations,

According to the Jafan Chronicle the. tite

late.. The rat ling at aver Y17 per Kokee, 
and the retall ‘pri ‘abe to over 
29.sen per sho which is said to be a record price

froni the poorer classes as {o the dearness of 
tice, Inannrtiglethe (aka Shimpo qhserves’

Osaka journal,,but the'general appreciation 
of commodities and the increase’ of Land-tax 
may be the “principal. factors. ‘Ihe failure 
‘of crops in Russia and Ching” may’ also 
be responsible in some measure for rise of the 
market, but. this, having no direct bearing on 
the corn market-of Japan, its influence 
oo .the Japanese. rice market may soon 
disappenr. It ia generally admitted that 
the: price: of-rice has been comparatively. low, 
and, that it was only natural that it should go 
up. The, advance in the price of rice will in- 
crease the difficulty of the poorer classes to get 
-a living, and will affect the country economical. 
ly, but on the other hand it willincrease the 
profit of the farmers, with the result of an 
entiancement in the value of land, which will 
pive an inducement to capitalists to inves 
their money in realestate. The price of rice 
forms 4 most important: question for the con‘ 
sideration of the economic circles of Japan, and 
it requires ‘very careful study. The improve- 
ment of the living of the masses and the steady 
“{ncrease in.the population wii sary ite 
crease the demand for rice, the outpul-of rice 
in Japan ‘will in timé’ become inadequate ta’ 
meet the demand, aod the importation of 
cereals will rapidly increase.” A large area‘of 
waste land still. awaits cultivation, and the 
method of tilling land: still leaves room for 
much improvement, go-that the advance: of 
the price of rice may hasten the cultivation of 
waste Jand and also bring about an improve- 
ment in the methods, of tilling the s

rapidly been formed, ‘There are now 21 new mill-

pipers the output of flour in 24 hours when these

at 5,480 barrels, or 21,921 bags, in addition to

rels each, When the output of the mill at 
Hankow, 400' barrels, is-added, the daily out-

demand, The principal mills’ “are the

‘The Colonial Secretary in moving the follow: 
"Resolved that the principle 
of the payment of salaries ‘of European Civil 
Servants contained in Lord-Elgin’s despatch No,

1 make this resolution, sir, fro furma, leaving 
* it ta the unofficint members of the Council to 
express their own opinion. on the. subject, 
The extra amount which will-4e required for 
the Girt six months of thi raleeady passed 
will” beabout $44) As ‘regards’ the | re*" 
mainder of the year—in fact for the whole year 
—the total amount required iv already voted 
‘as the Estimates were taken at the rate of 2s, 
Dr. Ho Kai: IT have much pleasure in se- 
eénding the. resolution, which: bas heen pro- 
osed by the hoo. gentleman opposite. The 
chon, the Colonial’ Secretary has proposed 
this fro forms. 1 quite appreciate his posi- 
tiot, inasmuch ar this motion deals with 
the salariesof the members of the public 
service, of which he is a member. ‘I'he 
question arose eighteen months ago, and at one 


"time figured largely in the public Presa and 


elsewhere. It culminated in May last when a 
series of petitions was bent. home to the Secret- 
acy of State regarding the positiop--the un- 
fo}lunate position—of the members of the Civil 
Service on account of the rise in the value of: 
the dollar, which placed them all in a position 
to fore “a number of dollars, while at 
the same time prices, rent and. o’her 
expen: had ‘not abated ‘in - proyortion 
to the rise in the value of, the dollar. Uns 
‘fortunately, the , Secrétary of State had not 
, seer fit in the first’ instance-to grant (hat prayer, 
bot as is seen by despatch 122, dated tli June, 
1907, the Secretary of Siate, Lord Elgin, at 
last consented, subject to statutory approval, to 
a certain arrangement which will give members 
of the Civil Serv'ce in this Colony some 
adeguate compentation for their iabours, As 
I said, sir, the subject was brought before the 
‘public some time ago, and I must ‘say vthat 
most members of this Council, and also a very 
+ great proportion of the: public at large, sympa- 
thise very greatly with the members ofthe Civil 
Service, and we thin an arrangement of this 
kihd, fixing the dollar at as, wifl be a. very 
fair setttement af the question, Werfeel that 
. with the increased value of the dollar that 
should there be any loss the Colony shauld pay 
' it rather than the members of the - Civil 
Service, J goon. the principle that the 
Colony has” a. right to expect good . and 
+ faithfal service from the. members of the Civil 
Bervice, ‘and .on their part that they have a 
right to look to the Colony to, give to them 
an adequate.amount commensurate with their 
services, and also toenible them to maintain 
their position of some scale of comfort. I have, 
theretore, very much pleasure in supporting 
this resolution, Tam sure that my unofficial 
“colleagues, in this Council will: unanimously 
support me in dealing with this. (Appiause),”

The Hon. Mr. May: 1. gather from the

vernment will of course, not vote on: this.

_ THE NAVAL STATION AT OLONGATO, *ye2 
The New York Herald's pian-for making 
Cavite and Olongapo the bases for a great fleet 
of battleship will find favour with every thinking 
person in these iglands. Here are we, perhaps 
th: greatest Power in the yorld—certainly the 
richest nation of all—represented in the most 
troubled of the seven ‘seas by as many cruisers 
as one can count on the fingers -of ons hand 
while our natural tradé opponent is withio 
4,000 miles with a dozen battleships, as many- 
-cruisess, und four score torpedo-boats,.to say 
nothing of an even hundred other warvessels.. 
» Tf our floating foris are for use they should 
be where. their work is most likely“ to be, 
“When we cast the hazard of our, fortunes sn 
these ultern islnnds we took’on ourselves 
the responsibilities of guarding them, We are 
shirking ‘our task if-we do not, and we.might 
as well have no ships of war here as what we 
have. Notthat they ave pot magnificent speci- 
mons of their kind, manoed by as gallant crews 
ag ever Codadeck; bit we have sent a boy 
on a man’s errand, and there is a full-grown 
man around the corner who may jump on him 
and take all he has in his pockets. a 
Unt.l Great Britain madé her secohd alliance 
with Japan, alter the close of che Russo-Japan- 
‘ese struggle, America, as Britain, waa repre- 
sented here by Battleships. True ours were 
rathér antiquated and they were few, Whes 
the Anglo-Japanese alliance relieved England 
of all necessity of watch and ward in the Pacific 
she took home her Jeviathans to guard against 
her envious neighbours in Europe.” America 
replaced her battleships witli cruisers at the 
same time, . # 
” Then things were differest. We had helped to 
make the peace of Portsmouth and Japan arid 
the United -tates were more thao friends: 
they were lovers. A cliange has come o'er the 
spirit of our dream. . The stage has been fully 
sei forthe great drama. ofthe century. “The 
Mastery of the Pacific.” 5 Ae ie 
That marvellous acidr, who though trained 
in the old scheal was able to cast aside tra- 
dition, and almost in a day nstonish all-the. 
layers of the earth by his abylity as a tragedian, 
has sprung: into the. all-star of the Far East 


Company with a delermination to be the lead- J. 


ing-imnn ks. : 
America having joined this aggregation must 
do her part: + he cannot lag-with credit ta ber- 
self nor can she affordto play any minor past. 
Fate and determination having ‘put us in the 
Enlee ines, Hawaii, Guam und Midway, we 
have them to hold, . 
A permanent battleship squadron here means 
also the immense accompaniment of. colliess, 
supply ships, etc which like saltelites revolve 
around. these suns. - It means also fasier work 
on the fortifications of the Philippines. - These 
islands within two years or three at the utmost. 
should be as strongly defended as is Nagasaki. 
Otherwise we must count near the overwhelm: 
ing chance of having them taken from us, 
"Aside from the patriotic spirit which seeks to” 
have one's own country show its might abroad,. 
to Ihe merchants of Manila the probability of a 
great fleet, of battleships stationed here must, 
‘appeal irresistibly.) ° we mm hy 
t’ would mean -huodreds. of dollars a year. 
spent for supplion of many kinds, = 
_. ft- would: mean’ the preseyce ocar.here of 
thousands of Americans who would add to the 
business of this port and city tremendously, : 
: Manila would thea have some.of the airs of 
Hongkong-where a score of warships fling the. 
Btitish flag to the brecré and. thousands of 
English jack pend theirearnings,.

2 aN '- SPECIRS OF RATS. = ; 
The (allowing are the species of ‘rats we have 
found in Jiongkong:—Mus rattus, decumauus, 
‘Mus musculus and the so-called “musk rat” 
which isnot a rat. but a. shrew “Sorex 
giganteus.” Bast e 
THE -DLACK RAT: MUS ‘RATTUB: * 
Although this is usually called the black rat 
‘it is ‘Beldom that one js seca ‘which is secally 
black and we have not seen one of this colour 
in Hongkong. The most- common colour i A. 
dirty grey. “The fur is usually fine and soft, ot 
-a lighter shade on the betly, and extedds quite 
a litle way along the begwaing of the tail; 
mixed with the fur are sometimes a few spine 
hairs, though these are often absout, The tail 
ie longer than the body and head together, it 
is generally slender and tapers to, a fine. point. 
‘The ears are moderately large, standing up 
distinet out of the far and.extending ‘o the 
eye and even beyond it when laid forward 


here ate. five :pads on-the fore foot aod six’, 


on the hind foot. The hindermost pads are 
elongated, the digits well, separated. and 
more delicate than in the decumanus, ‘the 
claws are” sharp, curved: and -adapted for 
climbing, THe skull is slightly: convex -above.. 
The iccisous are exceedingly sharp and ‘sloped 
on the wearing surface at a very acute angle. 
The inferior maxilla is broader, thinner and 
less rounded on its tower edge than that of the 
decumaous and the incisors follow the curve of 
its inferior border, abe whole build ofthe 
animal is more light and gracefal'thao that of 


the brown rat and is better, adapted. for. climb-} 


ing than burrowing. ; 
THE: DROWN. RAT! MUS. DECUMANUS, — 


There is little to. choose in colour. between 
this and'the black rat... Taking an average of 
colouring the brown rat is:the greyer of the’ 
two and of n-lighter shade and this is more 
noticeable when a number of the two are.ex- 
amined together.’ J he fur is.coarsaand on the 
back js mixed with longer and: more bristle 
like. hairs which have: a. brdwnish ‘red. tint 
‘these are continued.oo down the: tail which is 
tcaly and bare of fur alniost from the’ root, 
The tail is shoiter than the head and body .to- 

ether, it is. thick and coarse, scaly and ends 
‘n a compiratively blunt’ point. In man 
specimens the point has been ‘Jost throug 
some atcident, The ears are, short, rounded, 
set closely into the fur aud when Jaid forward 
do not reach to the other canthus of the eye. 


The feet are large and strong, the pads being | 


more rounded then in the rattus and covered’ 
with a thicker, tougher ekin,-and the digits 
are ‘shorter, stronger, more.closely” set to-"

4. Wa submit that in the ‘Public Service, 
there can be but one jost standard by. which to 
‘determine what is or what is not an. adequate 
recompense for the services performed. This 
standard ip based upon the.cost.of the manner 
of living proper'to the class of persons who 
render those services, and is calculated to main- 


* tain them in a reasonable degree of comfort. 


‘Our contention is, that our remuneration falls 
below this standard, and that it’ should, there- 


. 4 The fustice ‘of ‘this argument; that our 
: es must. be suited to the cost of'living of 
the day, has on several occasions been re- 
cognized by the Secretary of State. To mention 


two only, in 1804 what was known as “half |’ 
“" compensation,” and ‘in. rgot “double.- com 


peosation," were given.to.us. On both theso 


! occasions the ostensible reason for the measure: 
ofrelief was that the value of the dollar had’ 


‘fallen, ‘But the tre reason can only be, that 
the falling dollar had disturbed the conditions 
of living (o-our detriment, . Had it beea other- 
wise,‘ had we been unable t> shew, not only 


thatthe circamstances had“changed but also-| 2) 


‘ that the change had béen prejudicial to us, we 
should have been, totally unable. to esfablish 
a cate; : on oF 
 § J ion js no less serious; 
while the fact that it is attended by a rising 


“=——instead ofa falling dollar’ seems to prove, ‘not 
; . that our difficulties are imaginary, but that the 


‘ 


‘ 


price of silver is aot the sole factor to be.con- 
sidered in calculating the'tost-of living;.. 

* 6,.To determine the cost of living of so 
heterogeneous a body as the Hongkong Civil 
Service, and thence to deduce a fair rate of 
emoloment, would be a task of great difficulty. 

‘It might be done thoroughly by a Commission 

" with onlifited time at its disposal; any such 
thorough treatment is imanssible for us.” But 
as we feel that facts alone and not theories can: 
prove our case, we have endeavoured: to. 

* select two typical instances, and.to deal with 
them in a concrete way. - If our argument hold- 
good for them, it will tallow naturally that pro- 


. portionate ‘readjustments should be’ general 


throughout the Service. 
7. In making our selection of typical cases 
we were impressed by. the fact, that the ad- 
«ditional cost of.a married life is.far heavier, in 
proportion te that. of a single ‘man, here than 
at home. Jt is-dnnecessary to labour this 
‘ point—house rent and steamer fares’ alone 
would prove it. It seems inevitable therefore 
that our typical cases should be married men: 
It follows that they should also be men whose 
marriage.could not have ‘been considered by 
the Government as an act of imprudence, for 
the consequences of which ‘they were them- 
solves. alone to blame.’ Further, our examples 
_ had to be chosen from different grades of the 
Service. Again, as the up-bringing of children 


. ,ie an ordinary consequence ‘of marriage, we 


took for our typical cases officers on’ their 
highest increment, on the supposition that they 
bad married on first roaching their present ap- 
ointments; and we have seppored that. they” 
ave each a family of three young children, the 
increasing cost of whose later education may 


” "be left to tha future and subsequent promotion,- 


" The types selected by.us to answer all these 
conditions. were (A) the head of a junior de-. 
partment oo a salary of $5,400 with compen: 
tion nee (B) & subordinate officer. on a salary 
of £345. ae Sn

He was in Hongkong at the 

returned:from Spain and established an office

. THE BOXER INDEMNITY, 
REMISSION. OF AMERICA’S CLAIM, © 
A Boston despateh of 18th June says:+-Sir 
Chen Liang ‘Cheng, retiring Chinese ambas- 
sador to the United States, who is in this city 
ona visit, to-day madé the following statement: 
*+ shall make public today the contents of 
an ‘official: note which I.received from Secretary 
Root last Saturday night, which shows better 
than anything ele can the reMmarkably high 
sense.of justice which the United: States has 
used: in all her dealings with Chinas “You 
remember that «fier the Boxer troubles China 
agreed to pay an indemnity of $24,440,778.51 
on account of the losses entailed bythe United 
States government, as well as for personal pro- 
perty lost by her citizens, during the: Boxer 
campaign. : ge dee ot Oe 
Four years ago your government was good 
en ugh to. promise me that when the time, 
arrived, as a token’ of sincere friendship for 
Chinn, the original figures of the indemnity 
would be revised. So : wrk 
‘| PRESIDENT NOTIFIRS' AMBASSADOR. 
“True ‘o the promises of the executive offi- 
cers, 1 received a note from: Secretary. Root 
last Saturday ‘night saying that the president 
direcjed him to say that in his next message 
to Congress be would be pleased to recommend 
that China be relieved of all obligation in 
excess of the final: revised amount .of the in- 


demaity, which had been set at $11,655,492.€9. | 


That, as you can see, will save China over 
$12,°00,000 and also an interest of 4 per cent. 
‘You cannot emphatize too. strongly my 
great admiration for the fair 
always characterized the dealings of the United 
States with my country. . This final action is 
another monunient to,America’s high seose of 
ustice, and [ feel called. upon to. say—not 
ecause I am the retiring ambassador—that 
personally { feel that Amerita‘is one of China’s 
strongest friends.” | Pe aaty o t

atalments for thirty-nine years with interest. ;

“A Seattle despatch of .8th alt, says:— 

“Opportunities for American Trade Exten* 

ion in the Far East" will be briefly discussed 
‘in ao address by Fletcher S. Brockman, at the 
meeting of the Chamber of Commerce at 1 
o'clock this afternoon, eat . 

Mr. Brockman who hat spent more than- 
Gifieen years in China, much of the ‘time in 
Y. M. C, A..work, and has made a careful 
‘study of commercial conditions in that country, 
believes that unless ibe Pacific coast shortly. 
awakens to its neglected opportunity, France, 
Germany and England will have secured the 
trade that is koocking. at. the doors of this 
country. . Mr. Brockman, speaking ,of the 
subject in an Interview yesterday, said: 

“My. work during the past ten years bas 
taken me over. many parts of China, Korea 
and Japan, and has afforded an opportunity to 
observe the marvellous development which is 
now taking place in the Orient.-The- most 
significant and the most coldssal change which 
4he—world has ever witnessed in the same 
period of time Kas taken place in-China during’ 
the past two years. The indust-ial (and cau: 
sequently commercial), educational and gov- 
eramental life of 400,000,000'people is bein: 
rapidly transformed, . The. significance of this 
to the United States, and capecially to the 
Pacific coast, can not be overestimated. 

“No people of Asia will prove more worthy 
ofa high place in civilization of the twentieth 
centary than the Chinese, The foreign trade 
in China—that. is the. new trade of China—is

ACCORDING to the annual report of the I-hilip- 
pines internal. revenue bureau for the fiscal 
year just ended the consumption of opium has 
increased instead of decreasing ns the result 
of the Commission to lessen aod eventually 
eradicate the use'of the pernicious drug in 
the fhilippines,. During the fiscal ' year 
1906 the tax on the’ diug consumed was 
P81\424.89, while -this last’ year shows an 
increase of | P508,896.77, or: a total of 
P689.621.77. The total collections for the 
fiscal year were P12,748,963.91 as compared 
with P11,366.606.70, showing an increase of 
po tess than . P1,3%2,367.2!,. or 12 per cent 
on the total amount. ul of this, the col- 
lections for the city of Manila amounted to 
P202,202.70.. The main increase was from 
taxation on distilled spirits, cockpits, cedulas, 
opium, the land tax in the city of Manila, 
and the percentage tax paid to the city 
of Manila on its grass income. The municipal 
laxea: collected’ in Manila. amounted te 
2,668,033 as compared with P2,465,780.40 the 
year previous. , The San Lazaro estate also 
shows a very large increase Owing to the in- 
creased number of pieces of the property: that 
have been rented and to the increased rent 
that has: been paid by the tenants during 
the .yeat, « The income of the estate for 
‘the fiscal year of .1906 was  P16,044.50 
while that for the fiscal year just ended 
was P62,467.37, showing an increase 
46,422.87. The apportionment of the internal- 


revenue tax collected from all parts’ was as_ 


follows: To the insular treasury Ps,426,496.23; 
to the provocial treasuries P1,68 4250.53; to 
the musicipal treasuries P2,323,369.76." The 
cost ot collection of this tax was io a'l 
P655,302.09 Or @ percentage: of 5.01 centavos 
for every peso collected. oat

HONGKONG, SATURDAY, JULY 20, 1907. 


HONGKONG FOLUNTEBRS, 

. eg + 
Major Chapman, Hoogkong Volunteer 
Corps, is to be cofiplimented « on “the steady 
* fncrease of the. force under his command. 


“Yo the report to the gist March: last just. 


published the'total strength of the Corps is 
given as 2go, being an additivn of sixteen 
‘members over the total for thd preceding 
“year. * Satisfactory as this increase is, Major 
Chapman is confident that, now that the 
new Head quarters have been completed, the 
* total will increase during the next year, pare 
. » ticularly, if the new infantry company, which 
‘haa been discussed, is started, Another 
” gratifying feature of the report is that the 
establishment of the Mounted Lroop was 
-taised in July of last year to. 43, viz, 1 offi: 


"cer, i sergeant, 1 corporal, and 4o, troopers, 


The Reserve ‘Association is becoming in- 
creasingly popwar with the senior residents 
of the Colony; for it had on 31st March, 
1907, a membership of 248, an increase of 
18 during the past twelve months.’ The 


.¢ members have carried outa large amount of 


. 


rifle practice throughout the, year at the 
King's Park Range, Kowloon, which.is more 
accessible than the Volunteer Range at Tai 
Hang, and at the’ Peak Range which has 
, been recently opened. ‘Alter teviewing the 
work’ of. the year as, regards gun. practice, 
musketry course, camps of 
and competitions, the Commandant re: 
cords the gratifying facts that’ a sema- 
phore signalling class was. formed dur. 
Ing the summer months. “On February 
6th, 1907, the Corpa had the honour of 
parading and lining the streets on the occa- 
“sion of the arrival of H.R. H.-The Duke of 
Connaught, Inspector General of the Forces, 
The Troop furnished gn escort. A small 
Cadet Corps was started in. May;_1906,_with: 


~ .** boys from the Victoria British School; there 


are now 13 members,- They ,are instructed 
n—squad—drill-and~¢emaphore~signatting- 
They attended camp and many of them 
" have already proved themselves very eficient 
Signallers. ‘Mt is hoped that their numbers 
will soon increase; The new. Volunteer 
Headquarters were opened on December

A. Tokyo ‘dispatch to the Asahi credits 
A statement to a certain authority" on havat 
matters, relating to the dispatch of a strong 
“American aquadron to the Pacific. ‘Inthe 
opinion “of ‘this authority «the nows is pet 
of such an important nalute as is generally 


considered by the Japanese public. He points. 


out that the U.S. Government has changed is 
poticy during the past teri years, now attaching 
importance to its: naval force.” The, U.S, Gov- 


ernment has been turning its energies to the |' 
construction. of war-ships and promoting other | ' 


branches of naval work The U.S. Navy, which / 


occupied fifth or sixth sank'in the list of]: 


‘navies of, the -world ten years ago, now stands 
in the second sank. All these new war-ships 
have been built on the Atlantic coast, and it i 


only natural that they should bs distributed in’ 
other directions, as they increase in numbers), 


and there is therefore nothing surprising in the 
movement of the U.S. synadron, Ms doly 
significance is that it shows that the’ navy-is in 


training, as declared by President Roosevelt. 7. 


The. situation. of Alaska and the Pacific coast 
generally, with” Hawaii and the Philippines, 
demands the reinforcement of the U.S. naval 
force on the Pacific and the U.S. Government 
has been forced to send ‘the. squadron in 
response to this-demand, It ‘is a" mis- 
take lo associate the dispatch of the équadron 
with’ the diploniatic situation between Japan 
and ‘the United Staies, or lo it as a demon: 
stration against Japan. . 

The repori that the tquadron i is comipossd ‘of 
(6 battleships is regarded by this authority as 
incredible, He refuses’ to believe that the 
U.S. naval authorities would detach 16-battle- 
ships from the Atlantic squadron, .Moreover, 
. a cruise of such a Jong distance could hardly 
be accomplished.-by..a squadron composed 
exclusively of battleships. In his, opinion, 
the squadron. is composed of 16 war ships]. 
cf. different descriptions, Speaking from a 
strategical point of view, this. authority rer 
marks. that the presence on the Pacific or 
AUantic of the squadron about to be sent to 
the Pa: ific makes no difference to Jupan- it 
the movement of the squadron has any strate: 
gical significance, 16 war-ships cannot be 
adequate far the purpose ; at least 20 or 30 
ships would be required, ° The departure of this 
feet for the Pacific, therefore, is of no import. 
ant significance (rom a strategical point of view. 
It is evident that.the U.S. Government has 
na intention in sending out the squadron of 
making a demo-stration against Japan: ‘The 
authority quoted refuses to’ Becept the report 
‘that the #quadron will remain on the Pacific 
only a few moaths. He is inclined to think 
that the squadron will juin the Pacific fleet 
permanently, :

‘THE WHANGPOO CONSERVANCY. 


According to a Nanking despatch the Shang- 
hoi. Taotai has asked,the Viceroy at Nanking 
to find sufficient funds far the Whangpno Con. 
servancy by presenting the statement of 
-accounts, Commencing from 7th moon of 
riext'year to the 6th moon of the 37th ‘year of 
Kwanghsu there will be a shortage af 1,509,000 
taels per year. It it ptoposed to’borrow thé 
funds from the Hupu Bank and Imperial Bank 
as well as the native merchants, for .a term 
of rs years. . The Hupu Bank has consented 
to lend-one millitn taels but the Imperial Bank 
has declined, and the native merchants have 


not given any reply. On the other hand, owe |. 


ing (o the prohibition of opium smoking ‘the 
funds first proposed to be raised out of native 
‘opium. dues will become less annually and 
after‘ten years there will be no revente from 
this source. Thus, Viceroy Tuan Fang has 
asked. the Board of ‘Finance-‘to instruct the” 
Nupu Bank to give help in finding funds for 


| the Whangpoo Conservancy.—-Simeanpao. .

FORMER'U. §. MINISTER TO CHINA, 


: Pasndena, May 18, * 
Major Edwin H. Conger, former Minister, to 
China, and at one time ove of the most notable 
members of the United States diplomatic corps 
died, at the family home in: this city at 3.45 
o'clock this afternoon, No hope for his re- 
covery had been held out by the atlending 
physicians ‘or Ue pist_ twenty-four hours. 
Daily fora week past Mr. Conyer has gradually 
-growa peaker, and it was known this morning 
that he had but a few, hours to live, ‘The 
family was at the bedside when the end: came. 
Chronic dysentery was the direct cause of death. 
Edwin Hurd Conger was born in Knox counly, 
Ml, March 7, 1843. Fis boyhood days were 
“spent on a farm, and, after a common-school 
‘education, he eniered Lombard University at 
Petersburz IIl,, leaving with his degree in 1862, 
H-Conger-enlisted asa private-in—Company-I.,— 
One hundred and Second fIlincis Volunteer 
Infantry, and rose step by stepuatil the close 
of the Civil: War, attaining the: rank of Capiaio 
and: receiving from the President the brevet of 
.Major " for gallant and menitozious canduct on 
the field" He entend Albany law school, 
graduating with honours in 1866. He was 
admitted to the bar in his native State and en- 
tered upon active practice at Galesburg, Ill. 
Removing to Dexter, Dallas county, Ia, he 
was elected first Ceunty Treasurer, and sexts 
in-1880, began the first of two terms as State 
Treasurer. Next he was elected to the Fosty- 
ninth Congress, and retucoed to the Fiftieth 
i rst Congresses. i. 
In 8 1 President Hartison named Conger 
Usited States Minister to Brazil, and for four 
years he la'.oured in a successful manner to 
cement the relations between the two republics. 
President McKinley returned Conger to this 
Brazilian post.upon entering the. Presidential 
office. in March, 1097, follawing it"the next 
year by transferring him to Chin Z

SLE PROTESTS NY EXHIBITORS. 


Complaints ‘are being made concerning the 
result of the judges"inspection of exhibits at- 
the Tokyo Exhibition now open. Mr. Konishi 
Rokuyémon,.a manufactures of: photographic | 

i ent back thé prize awarded 
him for bis exhibit, . It seems that Mr, Konishi 
expected, to received gold medal, but contrary 


} to bis, expectation only a first.prize wat award- 


tocess of manufacturing: 
ig paper, he has started its 
nanufact pan, and the import of this 
paper, which anntially amounted. to between 
Y500,0°¢ anid ¥6g0,000,has beeneatirely stopped. 
For.this service, he considers that he deserves, 
to receive a gold medal. On the 6th instant, 
after: the ‘distribution. of prizes, Mr. Kofiishi 
had an interview with Mr. Shirari, Director of, 
the Inspection Committee, and endeavoured to 
learn the reason why his special exhibit had 
not been awarded w.gold.medal, Tho answer 
being unsatisfactory to.Mr,-Konishi, he with- 
drew-all his exhibits the following day. . 

The eiembers of the Toihei Yoga-kai (a 
society of oil-painters) also complain of the 
unfair result of the inspection, and have sent 
back their awards, al the same time issuing a 
statement to the effect that complaints have 
been heard of the unfair resull of the inspection 
of cil-paintiogs in the Fine Art Building of the 
Tokyo Exhibition.’ The artisis admit, that it 
‘is difficult to satisfy every exhibitor, as'opisions 
of the value of a work differ, but by the publica- 
tion of the result of the.inspection onthe 6th 
instant, it was clearly demonstrated that the 
Anspection had been conducted rather. in con- 
‘sideration, of the personal circumstances of 
circumstarices of exhibitors than’ the merit of 
the exhibits, entirely ignoring’ the real object 
-of the ‘inspection, ““Cuch’s practice is con- 
taminating the sanctity ‘of fice art, and will 
‘leave an abominable example for the future,” 


say the artists; and for these reasons, the prizes |. 


+ awatded were sent back.—/apan Chronicle,

THE: MAKING OF A. MERCHANT. 


: —_—— 


FORMULAS FOR SUCCESS. 
Twenty-nine. years ago 2 draper's assistant” 
opened a little shop io Holborn. “His capital, 
after he had paid a premium for his premises, 
-was. £4; the frontage. of his home was 16!t., 
‘ond his staff consisted of a colleague and a 
boy. The two men lived behind-the shop, and 
for sometime their'combined weekly expenses 
never exceeded 128, 6d. ~ ee 
To-day the little shop has grown to a palatial 


| block with a frontage of 160 ft, and a depth of 


4co fl, the capital has increased to £310,000; 
the one errand boy has multiplied {o. an aver- 
age of 700 assistants, and the name of the owner 
has become a familiar word in: many lands, 
There is the story of Gamage’s in a nutshell. 
How has (his growth been attained? Why 
‘has this mau gone abead in such fashion when 
multitudes fail even. to hold their own?:. One 
cannot be even for a few. minutes with Mr. 
Gamage without learning part of the answer. 
His build, his: minoer, and his speech all 
denote intense energy: ; . 
“You ask mé¢ my secret for business suc- 
cess?” said Mr. Gamage. “There is no secret 
about: it. Assiness to-d y ts attained by the 
same formulas as-it-even w .s— perseverance, 
reliability, unceasing ‘work, and originalely. 
Many people do not get on because they 
-do_pot_seally desire -to, - Then want to 
enjoy themselves and have a quiet’ life. 
Others fuil to succeed because they are afraid. 
-tolaunch out. They go atong in thesame old 
way, They want to-re.ch the middie of the 
stream, but they stand shivering on the bank. 
afraid to take the plunge. Now, in business 
you can only progress by venturing on.new 
things an { by taking risks. The man who is 
determined to safeguard every action beyond 
the possibility of loss will never do much. 
You must -venture—originate things for your: 
self, and ‘get .out of the rut if you wish to go 
ahead. oo] ae . . 
“There is another point. Opportunity pas- 
‘ses every man, but every man does not sec 
it until it has gone by.’ Some opportunities, 
once lost, can never be recalled, In my own 
¢ase 1 had at one time the offer of the lease of 
the premises next my own. To take it seeme1 
avery told step, but [ did it, Had | not taken 
that lease when it was offered me my business 


must have remained narrowly circumscribed. | 
iness com: |’
